Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.

It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.

I’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.

Even when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.

With parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.

And the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.

It’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.

I know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.

Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.

And then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.

So… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.

Because then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.

So I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s hard to separate them, honestly. The first thing that bothers me is the way it looks to me — like I’ll see the lesson video and my attention just locks onto that one side of my face. It looks crooked, or swollen, or somehow uneven, and then I can’t really take in anything else about the video. I’m not watching how I’m teaching or whether the lesson went well, I’m just watching that side of my face over and over.

But then almost immediately it turns into what other people must be thinking. I start imagining the students seeing it while I’m standing at the front of the room, or parents watching a clip or a recording and noticing it, or staff seeing me and thinking something looks off. It feels like they must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying. So it’s not only that I hate how it looks — it’s that once I notice it, I feel exposed, like everyone else must be seeing the same crooked, swollen-looking side and judging me for it, even if nobody actually says anything.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them, honestly. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them, honestly. The first thing that bothers me is the way it looks to me — like I’ll see the lesson video and my attention just locks onto that one side of my face. It looks crooked, or swollen, or somehow uneven, and then I can’t really take in anything else about the video. I’m not watching how I’m teaching or whether the lesson went well, I’m just watching that side of my face over and over.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into what other people must be thinking. I start imagining the students seeing it while I’m standing at the front of the room, or parents watching a clip or a recording and noticing it, or staff seeing me and thinking something looks off. It feels like they must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying. So it’s not only that I hate how it looks — it’s that once I notice it, I feel exposed, like everyone else must be seeing the same crooked, swollen-looking side and judging me for it, even if nobody actually says anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a medical evaluation to check if there is any physical or neurological reason that one side of your face might look crooked or swollen?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 8\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking about whether I have had a medical evaluation for a possible physical or neurological cause, which is not covered by the provided symptom profiles."}

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Body Dysmorphic Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them, honestly. The first thing that bothers me is the way it looks to me — like I’ll see the lesson video and my attention just locks onto that one side of my face. I’m not watching how I’m teaching or whether the lesson went well, I’m just watching that side of my face over and over.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into what other people must be thinking. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that properly. I haven’t had, like, a clear medical explanation given to me for it, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t gone through some full workup where someone checked everything and said, “This is why one side looks different,” or anything like that.

Part of me has thought about it, because when I’m looking at it over and over on video, it feels physical — it feels like there must be something actually wrong there, like swelling or some kind of unevenness. But I also get embarrassed bringing it up, because I worry a doctor would just look at me quickly and say they don’t see anything, and then I’d feel ridiculous but still not actually reassured.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve had a proper medical or neurological evaluation specifically for that. Not in a way that settled it for me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. But most of the time it feels like it truly looks that way to other people too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about one side of your face looking crooked or swollen, is it more about how you believe others are reacting to it when they see you, or is it more about how you see it yourself, even if others don't react the way you expect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but it starts with how I see it. Like, I’ll notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on a lesson video, and once I see it, I can’t really get past it. It’s not just, “Oh, that’s an awkward angle.” It feels like that’s what my face actually looks like, and then everything kind of spirals from there.\n\nBecause then I immediately start imagining everyone else seeing the exact same thing. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them, honestly. The first thing that bothers me is the way it looks to me — like I’ll see the lesson video and my attention just locks onto that one side of my face. It looks crooked, or swollen, or somehow uneven, and then I can’t really take in anything else about the video. I’m not watching how I’m teaching or whether the lesson went well, I’m just watching that side of my face over and over.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into what other people must be thinking. I start imagining the students seeing it while I’m standing at the front of the room, or parents watching a clip or a recording and noticing it, or staff seeing me and thinking something looks off. It feels like they must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying. So it’s not only that I hate how it looks — it’s that once I notice it, I feel exposed, like everyone else must be seeing the same crooked, swollen-looking side and judging me for it, even if nobody actually says anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a medical evaluation to check if there is any physical or neurological reason that one side of your face might look crooked or swollen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that properly. I haven’t had, like, a clear medical explanation given to me for it, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t gone through some full workup where someone checked everything and said, “This is why one side looks different,” or anything like that.\n\nPart of me has thought about it, because when I’m looking at it over and over on video, it feels physical — it feels like there must be something actually wrong there, like swelling or some kind of unevenness. But I also get embarrassed bringing it up, because I worry a doctor would just look at me quickly and say they don’t see anything, and then I’d feel ridiculous but still not actually reassured.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a proper medical or neurological evaluation specifically for that. Not in a way that settled it for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It takes up a lot of time, and it’s honestly the main reason I’m here. I know, at least part of me knows, that it may not make sense in the way I’m reacting to it, but that doesn’t really stop it.

It’s like once I see that crooked or swollen-looking side of my face on a lesson video, I can’t just move past it. I keep replaying it in my head, and I’ll think about how I looked standing there teaching, and whether the students were staring at that side of my face instead of listening. Then I start thinking about parents seeing recordings or clips, or other staff seeing me, and I feel like they must be noticing the same thing. Like it’s obvious, and I’m the only one pretending it isn’t.

And it’s not a small worry, really. It can take over the whole day, because even if the lesson went fine, I don’t remember it that way. I just remember my face looking wrong. I’ll tell myself maybe it’s lighting or the camera or just me being too focused on it, but it still feels real. It’s very distressing. It makes me feel exposed, like everyone is looking at that one side of my face and not hearing anything I’m saying.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past something about my face. I’m a teacher, and when I see myself on lesson videos, there’s this one side of my face that just looks… crooked, and sort of swollen, like it’s not sitting right. And once I notice it, that’s all I can see.\n\nThen I start thinking that the students must be looking at it instead of listening to the lesson, and then I think maybe parents notice it too if they see recordings, and staff as well. It’s like the whole time I’m trying to teach, part of me is stuck on the idea that everyone is staring at that side of my face. It sounds hard to explain, but it feels really consuming, and I can’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this facial concern often throughout the day, even when you're not teaching or being recorded?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just when I’m teaching or when I’m actually watching the recording. That’s where it really grabs me, because I can see that side of my face looking crooked and sort of swollen, and then it’s like it confirms what I’m afraid of. But after that, it stays in my head.\n\nI’ll be doing normal things and I’ll suddenly picture how I must have looked in front of the class, or I’ll remember a moment from a video and think, “That’s what they were seeing.” And then I start going over it again — whether the students were staring at it, whether parents would notice if they saw the lesson, whether other staff are thinking something. It’s like I can’t just see my face as a face anymore; I see that one side, and it becomes the whole thing.\n\nEven when I’m trying to focus on something else, part of my mind keeps circling back to it. It feels… exhausting, honestly, because I know I’m supposed to be thinking about the lesson or the work, but instead I’m stuck on whether everyone is noticing that my face looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about your face come up, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly — like checking your face in mirrors, taking extra time to groom, or trying to cover that side of your face — to try to manage how you think others might be seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I want to admit. It’s not just a quick check and then I move on. Before I post a recorded lesson, I’ll sit there and adjust the camera angle over and over, like if I tilt it a little higher or turn slightly, maybe that side of my face won’t look so… off. And then I replay the clips, sometimes just the same few seconds again and again, trying to see if it’s noticeable.\n\nWith parent conferences, it’s similar. Before I join, I’ll test the camera and shift where I’m sitting, move the laptop, change the lighting, turn my head a certain way. Sometimes during the video, I catch myself putting my hand up near that side of my face, like I’m pretending I’m just resting my chin or thinking, but really I’m trying to cover it a bit.\n\nAnd the recordings are the worst. If I see it looking crooked or swollen, I can’t leave it alone. I’ll re-record the whole lesson, even if the teaching part was fine. Then I do it again because maybe the next one looks worse, or because I notice it at a different angle. It can take so long that I miss prep work I was supposed to do, and sometimes I end up late getting to my classes because I’ve spent all that time trying to make my face look less noticeable on the recording. It feels ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it before anyone sees it.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your face in a mirror or a regular photo, without any video angle or lighting concerns, do you still feel that one side of your face looks crooked or swollen, even when others might not notice anything unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, the videos are definitely where it hits me hardest, because that’s when I feel like I can really see it — that one side of my face looking crooked and kind of swollen, like it doesn’t match the other side. But even if it’s just a mirror or a normal photo, I still end up looking for it.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight there. I don’t just see my whole face, I see that side first, and then I can’t unsee it. And then my mind starts going, “That’s what the students see when I’m standing in front of them,” or “That’s what parents would notice if they watched the lesson,” or “The staff must be seeing it too.” Even if no one says anything, I feel like they must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m actually saying.\n\nI know other people might not react or might say they don’t notice anything, but to me it looks really obvious. Once I catch it, it becomes the only thing I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how certain you are that one side of your face actually looks crooked and swollen — do you believe it truly looks that way to others, or do you think it might be more noticeable only to you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think I do believe it looks that way. I know part of me is supposed to say, “Maybe it’s just me noticing it more,” because people don’t always say anything, and I understand that not everyone is studying my face the way I am. But when I see it on the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel like a maybe. It feels obvious — like one side is crooked or swollen-looking, and once I’ve seen it, I can’t convince myself it isn’t there.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking, “That’s what they’re seeing.” The students in the classroom, the parents if they watch something, other staff if they come across the video… I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying. Even if no one mentions it, I keep assuming it’s there in their minds, like it’s distracting them.\n\nSo… I suppose if I’m being honest, I’m not very certain it’s only noticeable to me. I wish I could believe that. The students in class, parents watching a recording, other staff if they happen to see it — I feel like they must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m teaching or what I’m saying. Even if nobody reacts, I sort of fill in the reaction in my head, like they’re distracted by it or wondering what’s wrong with my face.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m upset by what I see myself, but it becomes unbearable because I’m convinced other people are seeing it too. It feels like once I’ve noticed it, it’s impossible that they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how that side of your face looks, is it mainly the idea that it looks crooked or swollen that bothers you, or is it more about how others might judge or react to you because of it?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them, honestly.
